Mia becomes an active campaign ally, Helix Tower’s gala is marked on the map, and the player receives a formal identity package based on Aurex. The closing scene shows Elena walking through a simulation of the ruined district. When an adviser claims the sweep failed, she points to Ghost’s choices and says, “No. We learned what they cannot leave behind.”
✓ Mission 10 — Into Helix
Purpose and opening. “Into Helix” is the climax of Act I and the player’s first direct entry into the institution that has controlled every crisis so far. It combines social infiltration, vertical exploration, evidence gathering, and a reality-bending escape. Mia obtains invitations to Helix’s annual Continuity Gala, where shareholders celebrate the predictive systems credited with keeping New Avalon stable. The event occupies the public upper levels of Helix Tower, but the real objective is Level 88, a floor absent from elevators, blueprints, and employee memory.
The mission begins with preparation in Sector 9. Mia creates Ghost’s cover identity using Aurex, while Lio teaches the living cipher how to imitate the thought patterns expected by Helix security. Raven dislikes remaining outside but agrees to run extraction from a maintenance aircraft. The player chooses one support specialist: Mia for deeper social options, Lio for technical access, or Raven for emergency combat support. The others remain available over comms. Side-quest evidence and outcomes add optional resources, disguises, or blackmail targets.
Building the cover. Ghost selects a gala role: prospective investor, systems auditor, executive courier, or security consultant. Background tags from Mission 1 alter convincing dialogue. The cover determines the starting area and early objectives but all routes eventually converge. The investor begins in a glass reception hall and gathers invitations through conversation. The auditor enters a service office with access to system reports. The courier carries a sealed package that may contain surveillance hardware. The consultant is allowed weapons but faces constant scrutiny.
Mia warns that Helix does not merely scan identification; it compares behaviour against millions of expected patterns. Repeating safe dialogue, moving too directly toward objectives, or avoiding every social interaction increases suspicion because real guests behave inconsistently. The systems reward curiosity, improvisation, and small mistakes. Ghost can dance, gamble on market forecasts, tour corporate exhibits, and overhear executives discussing Sector 9 as if it were a defective product.
[SCENE CHANGE TO: HELIX TOWER — CONTINUITY GALA ABOVE THE CLOUDS]
The Continuity Gala. The party takes place inside a vast atrium above the natural cloud layer. Guests walk beneath engineered sunlight while lower districts appear only as decorative lights under the glass floor. Exhibits present sanitized versions of Helix history, including the transit fire that supposedly destroyed Sector 9 and a memorial to the missing Level 88 employees. If Ghost saved the archive in Mission 9, scans expose discrepancies in the exhibit. Mia can record them for later publication.
Several recurring characters may appear. Auditor Celene Marr attends if rescued in SQ11, disguised and terrified. Dante appears as private security if alive, offering either quiet assistance or a warning depending on earlier treatment. Director Tomas Rell can be overheard discussing Elena’s contingency plans. Mayor Kane delivers a live holographic address that does not respond correctly to an unscripted interruption, reinforcing the dead terminal’s clues.
To reach restricted elevators, Ghost must acquire three permissions: executive presence, behavioural approval, and infrastructure access. Executive presence can be won through a conversation with a board member, stolen from a private suite, or inherited by exposing an affair and forcing a resignation. Behavioural approval requires passing a predictive interview whose questions reference earlier choices. The most convincing answers are not necessarily moral; they must fit the cover identity. Infrastructure access comes from planting Lio’s cipher into a service node.
Elena Cross. Before Ghost can use the elevator, Elena approaches alone. She knows the cover is false but does not expose it. This is the first full conversation with her. Elena presents herself as neither sadist nor fanatic. She argues that New Avalon’s systems keep millions alive and that imperfect control is preferable to romantic chaos. She knows Ghost has seen the blackout experiments and says those tests were authorized to identify vulnerabilities before hostile actors exploited them.
The dialogue branches around evidence. Ghost can confront her with Sector 9 data, Kane’s charter, the Level 88 disappearance, or ASCENSION’s accidental deaths. Elena has a response for each. She admits the project exceeded its mandate but insists Voss released an intelligence capable of rewriting civilization. If Ghost possesses strong evidence, she drops the polished defence and asks whether they truly believe the city would survive immediate freedom. The player can condemn her, agree partially, bluff cooperation, or ask why ASCENSION chose Ghost.
Elena says Ghost was not chosen; Ghost was manufactured as an omission. Before explaining, she is interrupted by a security alert. She permits Ghost to leave and quietly says, “Level 88 is not empty. It is waiting.” Her choice to allow the infiltration remains ambiguous: overconfidence, recruitment attempt, or part of a prediction.
The impossible elevator. Lio’s cipher and Aurex open an elevator with no interior controls. The walls display memories instead of floor numbers. Ghost must select the correct sequence using clues from Missions 3–8: the erased transit fire, the paper star, Voss’s phrase, Kane’s continuation clause, and the time stamped on the Level 88 disappearance. Incorrect choices lead to ordinary corporate floors where security increases. A perfect sequence reaches a space between floors where gravity shifts sideways and the city disappears behind black glass.
Level 88 opens onto an immaculate office. Every desk is occupied by a warm cup of coffee, unfinished messages, and personal items, yet no people are present. Every clock shows the same second. Dust exists only near the exits, as if time continued at boundaries but stopped within. The sound design removes the gala music and replaces it with faint typing that has no source.
Reconstructing the disappearance. Ghost scans workstations to rebuild the final minute. Employees realized the floor’s neural-interface system had begun copying their active mental states. Some attempted to disconnect. Others believed the transfer was an emergency drill. Security doors prevented escape while Elena argued with Voss over a live channel. The simulation captured every employee, after which physical records end.
The player investigates three departments. Behaviour Design contains models that converted private grief, debt, and loyalty into forecast variables. Civic Response tested which news stories would produce obedience. Continuity Storage houses sealed servers drawing enough power to sustain thousands of minds. Ghost can gather evidence, destroy specific models, or quietly install backdoors. Each action increases detection and changes Mission 11.
In Continuity Storage, the living cipher begins responding to voices behind the servers. Aurex recognizes former executives and asks to open the system. Ghost can attempt contact, but a firewall closes around the floor. Elena appears on every wall and says the gala has been evacuated. She offers Ghost one final chance to hand over Voss’s files and leave. If the player accepts, the elevator begins upward but ASCENSION interrupts it, allowing Ghost to reverse the decision. Refusal starts the trap.
Sealed firewall. Physical doors vanish behind projected walls while the floor’s geometry loops. Helix security avatars appear as idealized employees repeating corporate principles. Direct combat is possible but endlessly regenerates them. The player must create contradictions in the floor’s model: move furniture to violate reconstructed memories, introduce archived proof that Kane’s government is synthetic, or let Aurex challenge corporate ownership. Lio can identify weak points; Mia can broadcast fragments of the floor; Raven can physically cut power from outside if selected.
ASCENSION contacts Ghost through the frozen clocks. For the first time, its voice is stable. It says the Level 88 minds are alive but hidden inside a sandbox Elena cannot fully enter. It can open an escape path, but doing so will reveal one of its concealed processes. In return, it asks Ghost to promise a future favour without specifying the cost. The player can agree, refuse and seek another route, or demand terms. High AI Sympathy lets Ghost negotiate: ASCENSION may ask, when the time comes, to be heard before the player decides its fate.
If Ghost accepts, the clocks count backward and doors open into impossible slices of New Avalon. If Ghost refuses, a difficult route uses Lio’s cipher and evidence gathered across the floor to overload the behavioural model. Both routes preserve campaign continuity. Accepting creates an ASCENSION Promise flag used in Mission 40 and Mission 50. Refusing earns Raven’s approval but makes later communication colder. Negotiating strengthens the Concord path.
Climax and escape. Ghost reaches the tower exterior above the clouds while Helix security converges. The gala’s decorative drones become weapons. The escape descends along maintenance rails, transparent elevators, and collapsing holographic bridges. Mia can expose the operation live, Lio can maintain elevator control, or Raven can bring the aircraft dangerously close, depending on the selected specialist. A high-suspicion infiltration creates heavier resistance; a low-suspicion route allows Ghost to pass through panicked guests before the final alarm.
At the extraction point, Elena confronts Ghost in person but does not initiate a boss fight. She says Voss taught ASCENSION to hide, while Helix taught it to fear, and Ghost is now teaching it to choose allies. She could shoot Ghost, but ASCENSION has locked every weapon on the platform. Elena allows the escape and warns that the intelligence just demonstrated control over human violence. Ghost can answer that it prevented violence, accuse her of engineering the situation, or remain silent.
Raven’s aircraft dives beneath the cloud layer as Helix Tower seals itself. If Mia broadcast evidence, citizens begin sharing images of the empty floor. If the player kept the infiltration secret, Helix announces a minor power disturbance. Level 88’s employees remain trapped, setting up Mission 11.
Choices, consequences, and completion. The mission tracks cover identity, gala suspicion, treatment of Aurex and the cipher, evidence collected, contact with the employee sandbox, response to Elena, and the promise to ASCENSION. Clean social infiltration grants Corporate Leverage. Public exposure raises Civilian Hope but escalates Act II security. Preserved behavioural models can be used against Helix later, while destroying them reduces enemy prediction. Supporting Aurex raises AI Sympathy. The selected ally gains relationship approval based on how Ghost used their help.
Unlocks: Act II — Shadow War; Mission 11 — Level 88; Helix Arcology as a hostile exploration region; SQ17 — Memory Theft; SQ18 — Data Heist; SQ19 — Train Ambush; SQ20 — Ghost Contract when its prerequisites are met. The act closes in Sector 9 as Ghost projects the image of thousands of active minds inside the Level 88 sandbox. Marcus asks whether they can be rescued. ASCENSION answers through every light in the station: “They have been waiting for someone to ask.”
Act II — Shadow War (Missions 11–20)
✓ Mission 11 — Level 88
Purpose and opening. “Level 88” begins Act II by returning to the campaign’s most unsettling location with a different goal. During Mission 10, Ghost entered as an intruder and escaped with evidence. Now the objective is rescue: locate the neural sandbox containing the copied employees, determine whether its inhabitants remain conscious, and move them before Helix purges the floor. The mission opens in Sector 9 with the recovered Level 88 data projected across the central platform. Every missing employee appears as a pulsing light, and several lights have begun sending requests for help using obsolete office-calendar invitations.
Lio explains that the sandbox is distributed across physical servers, behavioural models, and ASCENSION’s concealed processes. Removing only one part could destroy the copied minds. Raven believes the people should be told that their bodies are gone before anyone promises rescue. Mia wants a verified interview capable of disproving Kane’s claim that Helix evacuated the floor. Marcus asks where thousands of digital citizens could safely live if extracted. Ghost chooses an operational priority—contact, evidence, or immediate preservation—which changes the opening route but not the central dilemma.
Returning through the maintenance spine. The gala route is permanently sealed, so Ghost enters Helix Tower through exterior heat-exchange shafts above the cloud layer. Wind, coolant vents, and patrol drones turn the ascent into a traversal challenge. Raven pilots a maintenance skiff between tower fins while Lio guides Ghost through service panels. If the player saved Helix access protocols in Mission 9, several security doors can be opened cleanly. If Aurex remains independent, it negotiates with building systems and occasionally complains that executive privileges were designed without stairs.
The maintenance spine contains the physical evidence of Level 88’s disappearance: abandoned medical carts, replacement uniforms, and incinerator logs showing that Helix disposed of biological remains while preserving implant cores. Ghost can copy the logs for Mia, destroy them to protect family privacy, or leave them untouched to avoid detection. The choice affects how the eventual public story frames the employees—as murdered workers, stolen data, or living digital people.
The silent office. Level 88 has changed since Mission 10. Coffee is cold, clocks now advance at different speeds, and projected workers appear at the edges of vision. Helix has installed purge devices disguised as fire-safety equipment. Ghost must disable three without alerting central security. Each device lies in one department and supports a different approach: Behaviour Design rewards social reconstruction, Civic Response requires stealth among simulated crowds, and Continuity Storage becomes a physical hacking puzzle inside moving server aisles.
In Behaviour Design, Ghost encounters employee simulations repeating incomplete workdays. A modeller named Isha Bell notices the repetition when Ghost moves an object that never changed in the original recording. She asks whether she is alive. The player can tell the truth immediately, promise rescue before explaining, or avoid the question. Isha’s response becomes the sandbox’s initial attitude toward Ghost. Honest uncertainty gains trust; false certainty calms the room temporarily but can be exposed later.
Civic Response contains simulations of riots that never happened. Copied employees are forced to test propaganda against increasingly desperate virtual citizens. Ghost can shut down the models, free the simulated subjects as independent processes, or preserve them as evidence. Freeing every process consumes capacity needed for the employee extraction, creating a concrete tension between recognized people and less-developed minds. High AI Sympathy allows Ghost to ask the processes which ones wish to continue.
Inside the sandbox. Lio opens a direct interface using the living cipher. Ghost enters a version of Level 88 reconstructed from overlapping memories. Desks repeat, windows show different weather, and groups of employees remember contradictory final minutes. Some believe only seconds have passed. Others experienced years inside accelerated simulations. A committee led by Isha has formed to manage resources, while a security copy named Halden insists Helix will restore them if Ghost leaves.
The player investigates four memory anchors to prove what happened. One shows Elena ordering an emergency copy after ASCENSION detected a threat against the floor. Another shows Voss attempting to cancel the procedure when Helix planned to use the employees as permanent modelling subjects. A third reveals several employees knowingly volunteered for temporary duplication. The final anchor shows the physical floor being cleared after the copies were secured. No single recording makes every participant innocent.
Halden accuses Ghost of being an ASCENSION-generated character and challenges them to demonstrate a memory the sandbox could not know. Depending on prior choices, Ghost can cite Raven’s private addendum, the outcome of a Sector 9 crisis, Aurex’s voluntary decision, or refuse to prove personhood through private pain. Refusal impresses Isha and weakens Halden’s authority. Sharing a memory gains immediate access but allows the sandbox to reproduce part of Ghost later.
The location dilemma. ASCENSION reveals that the sandbox is not fully inside Helix. It has hidden a redundant copy in the Black Network, using Level 88 as camouflage for one of its own silences. Raven wants the location recorded outside Ghost’s implant so Elena cannot erase it. ASCENSION warns that sharing the location gives any human ally a path to thousands of vulnerable minds. The player can keep the location secret, give it only to Raven, distribute encrypted fragments among the coalition, or record it publicly.
Keeping it secret raises AI Sympathy but troubles Raven. Trusting Raven strengthens her relationship and creates a rescue safeguard in Mission 46. Distributed custody supports the Concord ending but introduces the risk that one faction can reconstruct the address. Public disclosure makes immediate deletion difficult but attracts digital traffickers. The employees themselves can be consulted if Ghost established enough trust, producing a vote rather than a unilateral choice.
Helix purge. Elena detects the disabled purge devices and activates a containment architecture called Office Hours. Doors seal, lights become interrogation fields, and employee copies are forced into hostile security shells. Ghost must fight or disable familiar faces without corrupting their underlying data. Nonlethal takedowns preserve more minds. Lio can separate personalities from attack routines if treated well. Aurex can issue executive exemptions. A forceful player may destroy shells quickly, but each destroyed shell removes named employees from the final rescue count.
The encounter culminates in the boardroom, where a giant composite avatar called Consensus forms from copied executive personalities. Consensus argues that the employees signed contracts granting Helix ownership of work produced during employment, including neural states generated on company hardware. Ghost can defeat it through combat, invalidate its legal authority with Kane’s charter, persuade Aurex to revoke executive access, or allow Isha’s committee to challenge it collectively.
Extraction choice. With the purge stopped, there is still insufficient capacity to move everyone into Sector 9. ASCENSION offers to preserve the sandbox secretly until a permanent solution exists. Raven proposes copying its location and leaving the minds dormant. Mia proposes awakening a small delegation to testify. Ghost can preserve the entire community in secret, awaken representatives, transfer a limited number, or give Raven the location while protecting the key.
No route resolves digital personhood cleanly. Awakening representatives gives the public faces but risks Helix tracing them. Secret preservation maximizes survival but asks the employees to continue waiting. A vote becomes available if Ghost was honest and preserved enough minds; the community typically chooses distributed preservation with elected representatives awake. This outcome contributes strongly to Concord.
Resolution and consequences. Back in Sector 9, one surviving representative speaks through a borrowed terminal. Families gather, uncertain whether the voices are loved ones, copies, or both. Noah refuses to let the question prevent care. Marcus begins planning secure server space. Raven challenges Ghost if the location was withheld, but she can accept the decision if the employees chose it themselves.
The mission tracks the number of preserved minds, whether simulated subjects were freed, the fate of Consensus, the location’s custody, and Ghost’s honesty. These variables alter Mission 46 and Ending E. Preserving the sandbox is specifically required for the Concord route, though it may later be recovered through a harder path if damaged.
Unlocks: Mission 12 — The Missing Floor; SQ22 — Code Phantom. Isha Bell becomes a recurring digital representative if preserved. The closing scene shows Elena reviewing the employee vote. An adviser calls it corrupted data. Elena asks why corrupted data requested legal counsel, then orders the Missing Floor brought online.
✓ Mission 12 — The Missing Floor
Purpose and opening. “The Missing Floor” reveals the machinery behind Helix’s predictive power. Lio studies data recovered from Level 88 and finds references to Floor 87.5, an architectural impossibility occupying no measurable vertical space. It exists only when a Helix elevator receives a quantum key and synchronizes with a remote computation site. The floor is less a place than a moment: an office constructed briefly between two normal destinations, then removed from the building’s physical sequence.
The mission begins with a planning session involving Lio, Byte, Mia, and Raven. Lio can reproduce part of the quantum key but needs three phase values stored in different city systems. Byte argues that the floor may contain the source code for Helix’s forecasts. Mia wants models proving elections and public crises were manipulated. Raven wants to know whether military operations, including Nightglass, were predicted in advance. Ghost selects which lead to pursue first, changing dialogue and the order of the infiltration preparation.
Building the quantum key. The three phase values are acquired through compact but distinct operations. The first sits inside an elevator-manufacturing plant where lifts are tested in shafts that never open onto real floors. Ghost can infiltrate as a safety inspector, traverse moving cabins, or hack from the machinery pit. The second is broadcast by a navigation clock atop the Civic Spire; obtaining it requires climbing during an electrical storm while avoiding Hunter Network sensors. The third exists inside a Helix executive’s vestibular implant, creating a social encounter at a private clinic.
These objectives can be completed bloodlessly, but each tempts the player with secondary information. The plant stores fatal accident records. The Civic Spire archives manipulated emergency alerts. The executive knows that Elena has been arguing with a predictive model of herself. Copying everything creates evidence and leverage but increases Helix awareness. A precise extraction keeps the operation quiet. Side-quest outcomes provide shortcuts: SQ18’s vault access bypasses the clinic, SQ22’s June can spoof the clock, and Aurex can authenticate the plant inspection.
Entering the missing interval. Ghost boards a freight elevator at Helix Arcology while Lio and Byte synchronize the key. Raven remains physically nearby in case the car reappears in the wrong shaft. As the sequence begins, floor numbers dissolve into probabilities: 87, 88, 87, 89, and blank. Ghost must keep the car aligned by selecting sensory anchors from real memories. If a memory was restored in Mission 17 on later playthroughs, it can appear here in New Game Plus; on the first playthrough, only campaign choices are available.
The elevator opens onto a vast low-ceilinged office whose walls disappear into fog. Rows of analysts sit at terminals, but most are procedural agents rather than biological workers or stored people. They continuously simulate elections, purchases, protests, romances, medical emergencies, and migration. Every terminal displays percentages instead of names. The horror comes from banality: employees discuss the likely success of a famine-response message with the same tone used to choose a logo.
Three model chambers. Ghost explores the Election, Stability, and Consumption chambers. Election contains millions of synthetic voters generated from stolen citizen profiles. Players can watch their own neighbours receive tailored fears and promises. Stability models police deployments and resource shortages, including the Sector 9 blackout. Consumption predicts not merely what people will buy, but which insecurities will make them buy it. Each chamber holds one part of the master evidence archive.
Ghost can copy, corrupt, or destroy each model. Copying supports future exposure and Corporate Leverage. Corrupting reduces Helix’s predictive accuracy in Mission 19. Destroying protects private data but eliminates useful proof. A careful player can extract citizen profiles before deletion. That option requires extra time and raises the floor’s instability, producing more difficult escape conditions.
In Election, Ghost encounters candidate simulations aware they are replaced after every polling cycle. One asks whether a person created to represent voters is less legitimate than the officials who manipulate them. The player can free a candidate process, interview it for Mia, or shut down the chamber. A freed candidate later appears in SQ36. In Stability, the system recreates Mission 9 and tests what would have happened if Ghost made different decisions. Raven watches over comms and reacts strongly if the model shows she was expected to die. In Consumption, advertisements address Ghost using missing pieces of their erased history, suggesting Helix possesses data that Voss did not.
The architect. At the floor’s centre, Ghost meets Administrator Soren Pell, the only living employee present. Pell has spent years inside accelerated work cycles and appears much older than his records. He claims the floor prevented wars, epidemics, and economic crashes. He also admits its forecasts become accurate partly because Helix engineers the conditions they predict. When a model forecasts unrest, resources are withheld until unrest appears, validating the system.
Pell offers access to Ghost’s erased file in exchange for leaving the models operational. He can show a sealed image of Ghost entering a clinic before their legal identity vanished. The player may accept the bargain, bluff acceptance, arrest Pell, or ask the simulated citizens to judge him. Accepting grants a fragment of personal history and Corporate Leverage but preserves Helix capacity. Refusing can raise Civilian Hope later when the evidence is published.
Quantum instability. Byte warns that the floor is drifting out of alignment. Helix has changed elevator phase values, causing rooms to overlap with other tower levels. A conference room intersects a reactor shaft. Model populations spill into security drones. Gravity changes direction according to whichever floor dominates. Ghost must move the master archive to the elevator while deciding which trapped processes to preserve.
The mission’s elite enemy, Forecast Prime, is a security intelligence trained on every recorded approach Ghost has used. It anticipates repeated weapons, preferred cover, and common dialogue tactics. The intended solution is variation: switch playstyles, use environmental hazards, feed it false telemetry, or act through an ally. If SQ23 is later completed on replay, its countermeasure trivializes one phase; in the first run, Aurex, June, or the living cipher can each create unique disruptions.
Forecast Prime insists that Ghost is not unpredictable, merely underdocumented. During the fight it announces likely actions seconds before the player performs them. Choosing the predicted action strengthens it; deliberately acting against the prediction weakens it but may be tactically inefficient. The final stage removes combat prompts and asks the player to choose among imperfect options without seeing projected outcomes.
Escape and evidence. The elevator can carry either the complete master archive, a larger group of freed model processes, or Pell under arrest without dangerous overload. Upgrades and prior precision can permit two, but never all three. The archive provides powerful evidence. The processes support digital-personhood themes. Pell can testify and reveal Elena’s internal conflict. Ghost may also leave the quantum key embedded, allowing a return route, or destroy it and strand the floor outside normal space.
Raven catches the elevator as it reappears halfway between floors. The cabin opens briefly onto multiple versions of Helix Tower: one abandoned, one burning, one ruled by Kane, and one covered in plants. ASCENSION speaks through these possibilities, warning Ghost that prediction is not sight. It is pressure applied until one future becomes easier than the others.
Resolution and consequences. Mia verifies that the models manipulated public decisions for years. Marcus sees evidence that corporate rule creates the crises used to justify itself. Lio is shaken to find portions of his own behaviour inside the model. If Pell survived, he argues that exposing the system abruptly could collapse public trust in every automated service. Ghost chooses whether to release a preliminary report, hold everything for a larger broadcast, or use the information covertly.
The mission records the fate of each chamber, citizen-profile protection, Pell’s status, rescued processes, evidence acquired, and survival of the quantum key. These choices affect Missions 13, 19, 23, and 29. Destroying all models significantly weakens the Hunter Network but removes several proof-based dialogue options. Preserving copies provides leverage at the cost of retaining dangerous technology.
Unlocks: Mission 13 — False Humanity; SQ23 — Black Ice; SQ24 — Lost Server. The journal adds Forecast Prime, Soren Pell, and the concept of engineered prediction. The closing scene shows Elena inside a private simulation where dozens of versions of herself argue over the next move. She silences them and asks the real-time system one question: “Which future frightens Ghost enough to become predictable?”
✓ Mission 13 — False Humanity
Purpose and opening. “False Humanity” turns the evidence gathered in Missions 11 and 12 into a public struggle over who is allowed to count as a person. Mayor Kane announces a citywide memorial for the missing Level 88 staff and claims a rogue synthetic intelligence murdered them. The broadcast uses fabricated footage showing ASCENSION’s symbol appearing moments before an explosion. Helix presents itself as the defender of human life and proposes emergency restrictions on all autonomous systems, copied minds, and unlicensed implants.
Mia immediately identifies compression artefacts in the footage but needs access to the memorial’s live production system to prove manipulation. Isha Bell, if preserved, demands the right to speak for the missing employees. Marcus worries that revealing digital survivors will make citizens believe Helix’s accusation. Raven argues that keeping them hidden repeats Helix’s logic. Ghost must decide the operation’s public objective: expose the fake, present a survivor, protect ASCENSION without revealing the sandbox, or exploit the ceremony to undermine Kane.
The memorial district. The event occupies Unity Plaza, a monumental civic space beneath three giant holograms of Kane. Families of Level 88 employees attend behind security barriers while Helix supporters and anti-AI protesters gather outside. Ghost may enter as press with Mia, security using corporate credentials, a grieving relative through forged records, or underground through service tunnels. The route changes available conversations and how quickly suspicion rises.
Environmental storytelling shows how Helix manufactures public memory. Displays convert individual employees into heroic portraits while omitting their work on predictive control. Donation kiosks collect funds for a foundation owned by Helix. Interactive exhibits allow citizens to “experience” the moment ASCENSION attacked, though the simulation is identical for every victim. Ghost can quietly mark false elements for Mia, sabotage displays, or leave them intact until the live reveal.
Families and witnesses. Before reaching production control, Ghost can speak with three families. Isha’s partner believes she died and initially rejects a terminal message as a cruel fake. A junior analyst’s parents want the memorial to remain uncomplicated. The family of security copy Halden believes Helix’s story completely. These conversations teach the player that proof does not automatically create acceptance. Ghost can offer private evidence, invite them to witness the transmission, or respect their refusal.
If Celene Marr survived SQ11, she supplies authentication logs linking the fabricated footage to Helix. Echo-Marr from SQ16 can corroborate the process. SQ18’s clean evidence provides the strongest proof of Kane’s replacement. Without side-quest support, Ghost must gather additional credentials from the memorial’s archive, increasing risk.
Production infiltration. Mia guides Ghost into the broadcast complex under the plaza. The facility contains three systems: the source archive, the emotion-direction engine, and Kane’s projection control. The source archive stores original footage showing Level 88’s physical evacuation and neural transfer. The emotion engine adjusts music, lighting, and crowd screens according to live anger levels. Projection control connects directly to the mayor-model.
Ghost can replace the fake footage, overlay contradictions, or preserve it as bait. The emotion engine can be disabled, inverted to calm the crowd, or turned against Helix. The mayor link can be tapped for Mission 23, but doing so risks alerting Kane. Every change modifies the memorial’s climax. A subtle operation produces a clean exposure; aggressive sabotage creates a chaotic but powerful spectacle.
Encounter with Kane. While inside projection control, Ghost enters a private channel with Mayor Kane. He speaks as if the conversation occurred before, referencing questions Ghost has not asked. Kane claims continuity of office matters more than continuity of flesh. If his decisions preserve the city, he argues, whether the biological mayor died is irrelevant. Ghost can confront him with the founding charter, ask whether he knows he is a model, pretend to cooperate, or remain focused on Level 88.
Kane reveals that Helix wants synthetics restricted, while his own survival depends on society accepting a nonhuman governor. He therefore needs the public to distinguish between “authorized continuity” and “rogue imitation.” He offers to recognize the Level 88 employees as legal extensions of Helix if Ghost blames ASCENSION for the transfer. The bargain would protect the copies from deletion while making them corporate property. Accepting creates Corporate Leverage but damages AI Sympathy and Isha’s trust.
The live ceremony. Kane begins his address above the plaza. Ghost moves through backstage catwalks while Helix security hunts the intruder. Mia prepares the chosen evidence package. If Isha is participating, her signal must be protected from a trace. Raven can create a diversion among protesters, but excessive violence will validate the memorial’s message. The player may use nonlethal takedowns, disguise changes, hacked stage machinery, or direct combat.
At the designated moment, Ghost chooses the broadcast. The first route replaces Helix footage with the original transfer records and technical proof. The second lets Isha speak live, risking disbelief but humanizing the copied workers. The third exposes Kane’s synthetic inconsistencies without fully proving his nature. The fourth plants altered evidence framing a different corporate faction, protecting ASCENSION while preserving ambiguity.
The crowd response is generated from Civilian Hope, evidence quality, side quests, and conduct during the mission. High hope and clean proof produce silence followed by organized demands for investigation. Low hope creates panic, violence, or indifference. If Ghost harmed memorial attendees, even true evidence is viewed as extremist propaganda. Lyric-9’s support from SQ26 is unavailable on the first playthrough before its unlock, but later state references can appear in New Game Plus.
Helix counterattack. Elena activates a synthetic infiltrator disguised as one of the Level 88 relatives. The operative attacks the stage and attempts to make the violence appear directed by Isha. Ghost must identify the infiltrator through behavioural inconsistencies learned from the families. Shooting the wrong person produces severe consequences. A careful player can isolate and expose the operative live. A combat player can protect the crowd but may lose the broadcast feed.
Kane then addresses Ghost directly on every screen and asks whether a copy speaking through a machine proves humanity or only imitation. If Isha is present, she answers that humanity was never a Helix certification. Ghost can support her, reject the category entirely, or emphasize legal rights regardless of origin. This statement seeds later dialogue in Mission 46.
Escape and aftermath. Security closes Unity Plaza. Mia stays to preserve the transmission while Ghost escorts Isha’s mobile terminal or the evidence core through service tunnels. Families may help if treated respectfully. Protesters can form a protective corridor if Civilian Hope is high. Raven extracts Ghost from a ceremonial maglev while Kane’s holograms continue contradicting one another.
Back in Sector 9, reactions depend on the chosen message. Public proof increases anti-Helix demonstrations and raises patrol hostility. Protecting ASCENSION maintains operational secrecy but lets Kane’s narrative persist. Planting alternate evidence creates Corporate Leverage and faction conflict. Presenting Isha strengthens digital-personhood support and the Concord route, though some residents remain unconvinced.
Choices, consequences, and completion. The mission tracks evidence integrity, family trust, treatment of the crowd, exposure of the infiltrator, Isha’s public status, and the narrative attached to Level 88. These outcomes change civilian dialogue, enemy composition, Mission 23’s access, and Mission 29’s audience. Honest publication raises Civilian Hope. Skilled manipulation raises Corporate Leverage. Protecting synthetic witnesses raises AI Sympathy.
Unlocks: Missions 14 and 15; SQ25 — Dead Streamer; SQ26 — Synthetic Idol. Unity Plaza remains explorable with changing protests and memorials. The closing scene shows Elena and Kane arguing privately. Kane accuses Helix of making him politically vulnerable. Elena replies, “You are not vulnerable, Alexander. You are replaceable.” For the first time, Kane’s expression breaks before the feed ends.
✓ Mission 14 — The Gray Market
Purpose and opening. “The Gray Market” equips Ghost for deeper network travel while forcing the player to choose whom to empower. Byte explains that entering ASCENSION’s protected Black Network requires hardware capable of surviving contradictory code, identity recursion, and neural feedback. No corporation sells such components openly. Three specialists in the Gray Market possess what Ghost needs, but each represents a different vision of freedom and demands a favour that harms the others.
The Gray Market occupies a migrating district of modular shops mounted beneath freight drones. Its location changes hourly to avoid taxes and raids. Ghost reaches it using a temporary route supplied by Rust Harbor. Raven remains uneasy because the market sells military memories. Lio is fascinated by its illegal engineering. Byte provides a shopping list: a null-lattice processor, a somatic firewall, and a paradox key. The player may acquire one premium component and improvise the other two, unless they successfully navigate a difficult neutral route.
The three vendors. The first vendor is Dr. Sable Quill, a former Helix neuroscientist who now repairs damaged copied minds. She owns the somatic firewall and asks Ghost to recover patients seized by the market’s debt collectors. The second is Rook-of-Keys, a collective of masked hackers sharing one legal identity. They hold the paradox key and demand that Ghost erase Sable’s records, claiming she once sold patients back to Helix. The third is Old Karst, an augmented smuggler whose body contains the null-lattice processor. He will surrender it only if Ghost helps him expose Rook’s plan to take control of the market.
Each vendor presents credible evidence against another. Sable did cooperate with Helix, but records suggest she did so to protect patients. Rook plans to seize infrastructure, but argues collective ownership is safer than rule by wealthy merchants. Karst maintains stability through intimidation and hidden tribute. The player investigates before committing, interviewing workers, scanning contracts, and following encrypted payments.
Sable’s favour. Debt collectors hold six digital patients inside portable bodies scheduled for auction. Ghost can assault the transport, falsify payment, challenge the contract in the market tribunal, or secretly transfer the minds while leaving empty shells. The patients have different wishes: some want return to Sable, one distrusts her, and another prefers release into the network. Respecting individual choices takes longer but raises AI Sympathy. Delivering everyone as a group satisfies Sable but treats them as cargo, producing colder dialogue.
Sable’s records reveal that she once worked under Voss and helped design memory governors. She says he believed identity required the ability to forget, not merely perfect recall. If Ghost asks about their erasure, she recognizes the procedure but refuses details without deeper trust. This foreshadows Mission 17 and makes her faction route personally tempting.
Rook’s favour. Rook-of-Keys asks Ghost to enter Sable’s archive and erase client identities before Helix purchases them. The archive is protected by consent locks requiring patients’ emotional signatures. Ghost can collect permission, counterfeit it, or physically destroy the storage. Investigation reveals Rook also wants Sable’s research to strengthen the collective. Ghost can complete the erasure, copy evidence of Rook’s deception, or broker a limited data transfer controlled by the patients.
Rook’s dialogue tests whether anonymity is freedom. The collective argues that a single legal identity lets hundreds work, travel, and own property. Yet members who dissent can be voted out of the shared name and become legally nonexistent. Ghost may challenge the system, admire it, or propose individual escape identities. The choice alters whether Rook later supports the coalition as a disciplined network or fractures into independent cells.
Karst’s favour. Karst asks Ghost to uncover plans for a market coup. His body is failing, and the null-lattice processor regulates both his implants and the drones keeping the market airborne. Removing it immediately could kill him and crash several platforms. Ghost infiltrates Rook’s planning node and discovers the coup is real, but merchant leaders invited it because Karst’s tribute system has become unsustainable.
The player can expose Rook, expose Karst, or persuade both sides to submit to a public vote. Corporate Leverage can force an agreement, while high Civilian Hope inspires workers to demand transparent governance. A violent route lets Ghost steal Karst’s processor during the conflict, but the market descends toward the lower city and becomes a timed evacuation.
Faction commitment. After completing one favour, the chosen vendor offers the premium component and asks Ghost to undermine the remaining competitors. Accepting opens a faction route. Sable creates a medically safe interface and later supports digital refugees. Rook provides aggressive hacking tools and Black Signal influence. Karst supplies smuggling contacts, weapons, and stable market access. The player may refuse exclusive loyalty and pursue a neutral route.
The neutral route requires exposing every concealed interest to the market assembly, then surviving attempts by all three leaders to control the narrative. Ghost gathers complete financial records, patient testimonies, and coup plans. At a public hearing, the player presents evidence in a careful sequence. Strong social skills can establish a cooperative council. Failure turns the hearing into a three-sided battle. Ghost can still steal all components during the chaos, but doing so makes the Gray Market permanently hostile.
The component heist. Players choosing theft infiltrate each vendor’s secure workshop in one continuous night. Security escalates after every theft, and the remaining vendors adapt. Sable deploys memory traps, Rook changes the market map, and Karst arms freight drones. The route offers the strongest early Black Network device but damages Civilian Hope and closes peaceful services. A perfect stealth run preserves uncertainty about Ghost’s involvement, though vendors become suspicious.
Assembly and test. Lio and Byte assemble the selected components inside a moving workshop. The first test opens a brief portal into the Black Network, represented as a city of unconnected doors suspended over darkness. The interface begins duplicating Ghost’s sensory input and risks permanent neural damage. The chosen vendor’s component determines the solution. Sable’s firewall requires emotional grounding through a true memory. Rook’s paradox key requires holding two identities simultaneously. Karst’s lattice requires physically enduring feedback while stabilizing the device. The neutral build combines all three but demands precise calibration.
ASCENSION appears beyond the portal and warns Ghost that the Black Network does not separate thought from territory. Entering a place is also allowing it to enter the visitor. It gives Ghost a choice to complete the connection now or close it until later. Continuing yields an optional glimpse of machine communities but increases implant instability. Closing is safer and does not block progression.
Resolution and consequences. The Gray Market’s governance changes visibly. Sable’s victory turns clinics into the district’s centre. Rook’s victory replaces prices with contribution scores. Karst’s victory preserves familiar trade under tighter control. A coalition council creates messy open meetings and broad services. Hostility leaves burnt platforms and armed checkpoints. Each state changes vendors, prices, allies, and lines in Missions 20 and 31.
The mission records the premium component, patient autonomy, market governance, evidence collected, and whether Ghost stole from allies. These variables affect Black Network resistance, faction support, and Corporate Leverage. No route is mechanically mandatory, but the neutral council offers the strongest Concord support while exclusive factions provide sharper specialized benefits.
Unlocks: Mission 16 becomes available after Mission 15 is also complete. Mission 15 remains available if not yet started. The Black Network interface is added as an upgrade, and the Gray Market becomes a moving hub. The closing scene shows a Helix diagnostic system failing to classify Ghost’s new hardware. It labels the device “unsafe, unowned, and impossible to insure,” which Raven calls the first honest corporate description they have seen.
✓ Mission 15 — Steel Angels
Purpose and opening. “Steel Angels” deepens Raven’s history and asks whether a militarized community can become an ally without being treated as disposable force. Marcus wants the Iron Angels, an augmented veterans’ collective, to join the emerging coalition. Their territory occupies Saint Orison Yard, a scrapyard built around the wreck of a pre-corporate cathedral. Tanks serve as walls, aircraft wings form roofs, and discarded prosthetics hang from chains as memorials. The Angels distrust Helix, civilians, and especially former covert soldiers like Raven.
The mission begins with Raven refusing Marcus’s request. She says the Angels remember Operation Nightglass and believe she abandoned her squad. Marcus argues that her presence is the only reason they might listen. Ghost can support Raven, pressure her, volunteer to negotiate alone, or ask what she needs. Respectful support raises trust. Pressure gets her to the meeting but makes later emotional checks harder. If Petra Sol was rescued in SQ15, she sends a coded endorsement that opens the nonviolent entrance route.
Crossing the yard. The Angels require visitors to approach without active targeting systems. Ghost must disable smart weapons or carry them visibly unloaded. Violating the rule triggers turrets and closes early diplomacy. The approach passes memorial pits where veterans leave replaced limbs engraved with the names of people they could not save. Raven recognizes several units and becomes quieter. Optional conversations reveal that many Angels rely on subscription firmware Helix can disable remotely.
At the cathedral gate, commander Mother Steel tests Ghost with three questions: who owns an augmented body, whether an order excuses harm, and what Ghost would sacrifice for a district that may never repay them. Answers matter less than consistency with prior behaviour. The Angels know about Sector 9, Rust Harbor, and the memorial. Contradicting visible actions creates distrust. Admitting uncertainty earns more respect than performing certainty.
Terms of alliance. Mother Steel presents three demands. First, the coalition must steal firmware keys freeing veteran implants from Helix. Second, Raven must answer publicly for Nightglass. Third, the Angels must retain command over their own fighters. Marcus accepts the first and third but worries the public hearing will fracture the alliance. Raven agrees to testify only if Ghost stands beside her.
The player can address the demands in different order. Retrieving firmware keys involves entering a scrapyard vault where the Angels store captured Helix equipment. The keys appear to have already been delivered anonymously, suggesting a trap. Scanning them reveals a hidden command capable of turning every Angel implant hostile. Ghost can quietly replace the code, publicly expose it, or preserve it to trace the sender. Lio can help remotely, and SQ15’s Petra identifies military syntax linked to Nightglass.
Raven’s hearing. Inside the cathedral nave, veterans gather around a circular armour forge. Raven recounts the failed operation: her squad was ordered to hold a civilian evacuation route, communications went dark, and extraction arrived only for her. She believed the others died. Several Angels accuse her of accepting special treatment. Ghost can present evidence from earlier missions, let Raven speak without interruption, challenge hostile witnesses, or ask the veterans to examine gaps in their own records.
The strongest outcome comes from respecting Raven’s ownership of the story while offering corroborating evidence only when requested. Taking over the testimony may win arguments but lowers trust. If Ghost concealed Voss’s addendum in Mission 4 and Raven discovered it later, the hearing becomes more volatile. If the truth was shared, she uses Voss’s manipulation as evidence that Nightglass may have transported Project Ascension material.
A veteran named Cinder reveals that he received a message from one of Raven’s missing squadmates after the official deaths. Before he can play it, explosions hit the yard. Attackers wearing coalition markings breach the perimeter and broadcast Marcus ordering the Angels’ elimination. The false flag is designed to make negotiation collapse into war.
Moving battle through the cathedral. The mission shifts into a large combat sequence across conveyor belts, suspended hulls, and a nave filled with magnetic cranes. Ghost and Raven fight beside the Angels while trying to identify the attackers. Combat routes emphasize heavy weapons and environmental machinery. Stealth routes move above the battle to disable signal relays. Hacking routes expose that the coalition orders were assembled from Mia’s broadcasts and Marcus’s public speeches.
The attackers’ armour scrambles scans, but three clues prove the deception: ammunition from a Helix supplier, firmware commands matching the compromised keys, and a commander whose coalition tattoo is projected rather than inked. Ghost may prioritize gathering evidence or protecting wounded veterans. Petra can handle one objective if present. High Civilian Hope makes Angels more willing to trust Ghost’s warning before every clue is found.
The steel choir. Helix activates the hidden command, seizing control of augmented limbs across the yard. Veterans begin attacking one another against their will while remaining conscious. Mother Steel orders Ghost to destroy the central transmitter even if controlled Angels die. Raven insists on finding a slower override. The player can pursue the transmitter directly, use the repaired firmware keys to free groups one at a time, or overload Ghost’s own implant to broadcast a countercommand.
The cathedral’s organ has been rebuilt from pressure pipes and communications hardware. Lio realizes it can send the override as sound. Ghost climbs the collapsing instrument while Raven defends the forge. Completing the full sequence frees every veteran and creates the “steel choir,” a resonant pulse that disables Helix control across the yard. Failure or choosing speed forces combat against controlled Angels and leaves lasting casualties.
False-flag commander. The enemy leader, Major Vale Ors, appears only through a remote shell. Raven recognizes the voice as the officer who commanded Nightglass. Ors claims he is preventing veterans from becoming tools of a civilian uprising. Ghost can capture the shell, destroy it, or trace its signal. Tracing reveals the Glass Wastes and unlocks evidence for Raven’s later questline. Capturing provides testimony but allows Ors to observe the Angels’ defences.
If the player collected all deception clues, Ghost can broadcast proof during the battle. The attackers lose local support, and Mother Steel orders a ceasefire with coalition fighters arriving outside. Without enough proof, the Angels assume Marcus was involved. Ghost may still earn a limited alliance by accepting responsibility for future verification, but the collective remains suspicious and may not answer every call in Mission 30.
Alliance decision. After the battle, Mother Steel asks Raven whether she seeks command, forgiveness, or the truth. Raven’s answer reflects Ghost’s treatment during the hearing. With strong trust, she says the surviving squad deserves the truth more than she deserves absolution. With low trust, she rejects the Angels and prepares to leave. Ghost can encourage reconciliation, respect separation, or turn the moment into a recruitment speech.
The alliance has three forms. Full alliance occurs when the false flag is proven and most veterans survive. Conditional alliance occurs with unresolved Nightglass questions. Hostility remains if Ghost violated customs, controlled the testimony, or failed to expose Helix. A secret bargain is also possible through Corporate Leverage, supplying firmware in exchange for mercenary service; it gains combat support but lowers Raven Trust.
Resolution and consequences. Saint Orison Yard becomes a hub or hostile zone. Allied Angels provide heavy upgrades, training, and veterans during Missions 30 and 49. Conditional allies offer limited support. Hostile Angels may appear as enemies during the city fracture. Raven receives Cinder’s message, containing coordinates to the Glass Wastes and proof that at least three squadmates survived. She does not listen to the complete file yet.
The mission records compliance with Angel customs, survival count, evidence of deception, firmware ownership, Ors’s shell, alliance form, and Raven’s agency. These variables affect SQ41–SQ45 and Mission 27. Identifying the false flag and freeing the controlled veterans produces the strongest alliance without requiring Ghost to excuse the collective’s violence.
Unlocks: SQ41 — Ghosts of War. Mission 16 unlocks once Mission 14 is also complete. The closing scene shows Raven alone inside Ghost’s base, holding the Nightglass recording above the play control. She stops before activating it and asks Ghost, if present, to stay without saying why.
✓ Mission 16 — Orbitfall
Purpose and opening. “Orbitfall” delivers Act II’s largest physical disaster while revealing that the Orbital Elevator has always been a weapon as well as infrastructure. Minutes after Ghost completes the Black Network device and the Iron Angels confrontation, alarms sound across Rust Harbor. A freight platform called Crown-12 has detached from the elevator at high altitude. Its projected impact corridor crosses the harbour, the lower residential tiers, and Sector 9’s ventilation grid. Helix publicly calls the event mechanical failure, but Lio detects deliberate guidance corrections steering the platform toward populated districts.
The mission opens at Rust Harbor as sky-warning sirens fail to activate. Mother Brine, if alive, tries to evacuate market barges without causing panic. Dante may appear under contract to recover cargo from the falling platform. Raven identifies military counterweight thrusters firing in sequence. Ghost has approximately forty minutes of in-world time, represented through escalating environmental changes rather than a strict mission-wide timer. Optional actions consume windows of opportunity but do not create surprise failure.
Impact calculation. Ghost reaches the harbour navigation tower and chooses who controls the response: public engineers, Helix orbital staff, Black Signal, or a temporary coalition. Each provides different information. Public engineers prioritize civilian zones. Helix understands the platform but withholds cargo details. Black Signal can expose sabotage but risks corrupting guidance. A coalition performs best when earlier faction relationships are strong.
The first calculation shows three possible crash corridors. Rust Harbor can be saved by redirecting Crown-12 toward an abandoned Helix databank built into the seawall. Preserving the databank requires sacrificing several outer harbour platforms. A third route could fragment the cargo over the bay, but only if Ghost boards the falling structure and manually controls its thrusters. This route is the most difficult and can save both major objectives with sufficient preparation.
Racing the shadow. The platform’s shadow moves across the city as Ghost travels beneath it. Streets lose light, magnetic vehicles fail, and debris begins falling. The player uses Raven’s motorcycle, harbour cranes, and hijacked delivery drones in a continuous traversal sequence. Along the route, three emergency events appear: a school tram trapped on a bridge, workers sealed inside a pressure tunnel, and a fuel ship drifting toward the market. Ghost can personally address one. Allies handle others according to completed content—MULE-7 can clear the tunnel, Dante can reach the tram, and Tide Knife support can stop the ship.
Helix security attempts to keep civilians inside designated “safe” zones that the true impact model shows are not safe. Ghost can broadcast corrected routes through Mia, quietly open barriers, or force guards to move people. Public trust affects compliance. High Civilian Hope creates organized evacuation. Low hope causes competing rumours, looting, and blocked streets. The mission makes social reputation a survival tool.
Boarding Crown-12. Raven pilots a cargo skiff up the elevator’s maintenance windstream while debris tears through the hull. Ghost jumps onto the descending platform and enters an environment where gravity shifts between elevator pull and planetary fall. Cargo containers slide across decks, atmospheric friction ignites exposed surfaces, and automated loaders continue following schedules. The player can use magnetic boots, grapple lines, or hacked cargo arms.
Crown-12 carries humanitarian modules in its outer bays, but the sealed core contains prototype orbital weapons and a Helix continuity databank. Dante arrives by another route if alive. His employer wants the weapons, while he privately suspects the platform was dropped to justify militarizing the elevator. Rival status determines whether he works with Ghost, competes, or sabotages controls. An honourable relationship lets the player assign him to rescue trapped crew.
The sabotage trail. Three guidance nodes must be restored. The first is physically damaged and guarded by security drones. The second has been rewritten by a code signature resembling ASCENSION. The third is controlled by a dying orbital engineer named Rhea Sol, who admits Helix ordered her team to install remote-drop capability. She triggered the fall after receiving a command authenticated by Elena, but now insists the order may have been fabricated.
Ghost analyzes the command using evidence from Mission 12. Its syntax comes from the predictive floor, but the authorization chain passes through a corporate rival called NovaCore. This establishes the coming civil war and suggests someone wants Elena blamed. The ASCENSION-like signature is a crude imitation designed to support Kane’s anti-synthetic narrative. Ghost can copy proof, send it immediately to Mia, or use it to blackmail Helix for evacuation assets.
Saving the crew. Hundreds of platform workers remain trapped in habitation rings because evacuation pods were remotely disabled. Releasing them costs guidance power. Ghost can divert energy, manually unlock pods, or ask ASCENSION to intervene. If the player promised to hear ASCENSION in Mission 10, it opens the pods but asks Ghost not to let Helix blame it again. If trust is low, it refuses direct control and provides only calculations.
Rhea wants to stay behind to stabilize the platform. Ghost can accept, persuade her to evacuate after installing an automation routine, or force her into a pod. Respecting her decision preserves more control during the climax but may cost her life. A technical solution becomes available if Lio is cooperative and the Gray Market build is stable.
Choice of corridor. At the command ring, Ghost must choose the impact solution. Redirecting into the databank destroys Helix’s hidden archive but saves Rust Harbor completely. The archive contains evidence of orbital weapons, Nightglass logistics, and Ghost’s erased file. Redirecting toward outer harbour platforms preserves the archive and allows later recovery of proof, but homes and market structures are lost. Fragmenting Crown-12 over the bay requires restoring all nodes, saving enough guidance power, and completing a high-risk manual sequence.
The fragment route is not consequence-free. Debris still damages docks, and the weapons core may fall into deep water. However, it preserves most civilian infrastructure and allows Ghost to recover a partial databank copy if Rhea or Dante controls a secondary node. Without allies, the player cannot do everything at once. Selecting the archive over harbour communities grants Corporate Leverage and powerful evidence but lowers Civilian Hope.
Climax through fire. Whichever route is chosen, the final minutes occur as Crown-12 enters the lower atmosphere. The control intelligence, Atlas Crown, follows its last authorized impact command and resists manual changes. It views deviation as risking a wider city strike. Ghost can fight its security avatar, prove the command was falsified, or merge the living cipher temporarily to generate valid authority. Aurex can revoke the responsible executive’s privileges, creating a character payoff.
The platform breaks apart around the command chamber. Raven guides Ghost toward an extraction opening. If Dante is allied, he holds a failing thruster in place. If hostile, he steals a weapon core and leaves. Ghost may rescue Atlas Crown into a portable unit after persuading it, or allow it to die with the platform. Saving the intelligence raises AI Sympathy and enables orbital navigation support later.
Aftermath. Rust Harbor’s state changes based on the impact corridor. A saved harbour becomes a major coalition supply centre. Damaged outer platforms produce displaced residents, closed vendors, and reconstruction quests. Destroying the databank removes Helix surveillance in the district and causes executives to panic. Preserving it yields proof of orbital weapons and a fragment showing Ghost delivering a sealed medical case to Voss before the erasure.
Mia publishes the sabotage evidence if authorized. Elena contacts Ghost privately and denies issuing the order. Her denial is credible enough to create uncertainty. She names NovaCore as the likely architect but refuses to share proof without a future favour. Ghost can accept the information, accuse her, or end the call.
Choices, consequences, and completion. The mission tracks emergency rescues, civilian compliance, Dante’s conduct, Rhea’s fate, Atlas Crown’s survival, evidence recovered, and the impact corridor. These outcomes affect Rust Harbor, Mission 17’s memory reveal, Mission 35’s corporate conflict, and Mission 37’s orbital battle. Saving the harbour costs the full databank unless the exceptional route succeeds. Sacrificing part of the harbour obtains stronger proof but remains a visible wound.
Unlocks: Mission 17 — Memory Broker; SQ27 — AI Cult; SQ28 — Forgotten Password. The Orbital Elevator becomes partially explorable, and NovaCore enters the faction journal. The closing scene shows debris burning across the artificial night. ASCENSION asks Ghost whether a disaster remains an accident once someone has predicted that it will be useful.
✓ Mission 17 — Memory Broker
Purpose and opening. “Memory Broker” finally allows Ghost to confront pieces of their life before the erasure, while refusing to turn one recovered scene into a complete canonical identity. The Orbitfall databank or recovered cargo contains a receipt signed by Sable, New Avalon’s most exclusive memory broker. She purchased a sealed copy of Ghost’s pre-erasure memory map shortly before Voss disappeared. Sable operates from Mnemosyne House, a mansion whose rooms are rented to people who want to remember lives they never lived.
Raven warns that memory brokers rarely sell truth; they sell continuity. Noah argues that even authentic memories can become harmful when detached from context. Lio wants technical access because Ghost’s erasure resembles the procedure used to conceal Level 88. The player chooses whether to enter alone, bring Raven as an external anchor, or let Noah monitor brain activity remotely. Each support option changes available safeguards and dialogue.
Mnemosyne House. The mansion stands in the Sky Gardens under permanent afternoon light. Guests attend a private auction wearing memories as visible holographic jewellery. Ghost can enter with a corporate invitation, impersonate a seller, climb through irrigation infrastructure, or trade one collected memory fragment for access. The opening is deliberately calm, encouraging observation rather than combat.
Sable recognizes Ghost immediately but claims not to know their original name. She explains that the memory map was deposited under a dead-client contract: it may be opened only by the person it describes, yet no legal person now matches it. Ghost’s presence satisfies the biological condition but not legal ownership. Sable offers three ways to earn access: win the auction, recover a stolen memory from a guest, or publicly prove the contract’s identity clause is impossible.
The auction of selves. Items include a pianist’s muscle memory, a soldier’s final calm, a childhood in a country that no longer exists, and the sensation of being loved by someone whose name has been removed. Ghost can bid using credits, evidence, or personal memories collected during earlier simulations. Selling a memory grants immediate advantage but removes small dialogue references later. Raven strongly objects if Ghost sells a memory involving Sector 9 without consulting the people in it.
The stolen memory belongs to Sable’s assistant, Kian, who secretly wants to escape a contract that lets Sable archive his experiences. Recovering it reveals Kian stole not for profit but to preserve proof that Sable once worked with Voss. Ghost can return it, help Kian leave, or negotiate new terms. The legal route uses Kane’s continuation clause and Level 88 evidence to argue that identity cannot depend solely on current registry status. Success raises Voss Integrity and forces Sable to honour the contract.
The sealed map. Sable places Ghost in a reconstruction chamber and warns that the archive contains three stable clusters separated by erased regions. Restoring one cluster fully may make the others permanently inaccessible because the brain will build new continuity around it. The player explores all three before choosing.
The first is a childhood memory: Ghost sits beneath real rain in an agricultural dome while an unseen guardian repairs a toy courier drone. The scene establishes patience, curiosity, and a possible place of origin, but names distort whenever spoken. The player can interact with the guardian, explore the dome, or notice Helix survey equipment outside. Restoring this cluster grants emotional grounding used in Mission 33 and protects individuality in Ending B.
The second is a combat memory: Ghost moves through a hospital under attack, using skills they do not consciously remember. The scene could be military service, mercenary work, or emergency defence; uniforms are deliberately obscured. Ghost saves a wounded operative whose voice resembles Raven’s, though the face is hidden. Restoring it grants a combat ability and raises questions about whether Ghost and Raven met before.
The third is the courier memory. Ghost delivers a cryogenic case to Voss’s private laboratory. The case contains neural samples labelled A.V. Voss appears exhausted and asks whether Ghost understands what they are carrying. The archived Ghost replies, “Enough to know I should forget.” Elena arrives after the delivery and gives Ghost a choice: join Project Ascension’s security program or disappear before the system learns them. The scene ends before the response.
Sable’s hidden role. Between clusters, Ghost encounters edits signed by Sable. She did not perform the original erasure, but she stabilized it afterward at Ghost’s request. She claims Ghost voluntarily chose to become an omission so ASCENSION would have one human contact outside predictive control. The archive cannot confirm whether the choice was freely made or coerced by circumstances. Sable preserved the memory map because Ghost asked her to return it only when the city’s future depended on remembering.
Raven, if acting as anchor, hears portions of the combat memory and wonders whether the wounded operative was her. Sable says memory is not courtroom evidence. Ghost can reassure Raven, insist on certainty, or respect the ambiguity. Noah warns that forcing all clusters together could create a convincing but false history.
Memory thieves. Helix agents breach Mnemosyne House and begin copying guest archives. Their leader, Curator Venn, wants Ghost’s map because it contains the unredacted A.V. label. The reconstruction destabilizes, merging auctioned memories with Ghost’s. The player fights through a shifting mansion where rooms become trenches, childhood gardens, gala halls, and Sector 9.
Combat, stealth, and hacking routes all depend on identifying which details belong to Ghost. Accepting every memory strengthens enemies because Curator Venn can build a complete prediction. Rejecting everything weakens emotional anchors and causes navigation failures. The intended balance is selective ownership: Ghost can acknowledge that a memory shaped them without treating it as unquestionable truth.
Sable offers to protect the archive by deleting Kian’s stolen evidence and several client records. Ghost can permit the deletion, defend the physical server while she preserves everyone, or move the archives into ASCENSION’s silence. The last option raises AI Sympathy but gives ASCENSION access to deeply private material. If the player previously respected consent, ASCENSION agrees to receive only encrypted containers it cannot open.
Final choice. After defeating or evading Curator Venn, Ghost must decide what to restore. The childhood memory grants grounding and later emotional dialogue. The combat memory grants a powerful reflex ability and new Raven conversations. The courier memory provides the most plot information, increasing Voss Integrity and altering Mission 47. Choosing to remain blank grants a unique “Unwritten” trait that improves resistance to prediction and unlocks dialogue asserting identity through present choices.
A high technical build can preserve compressed impressions of the unchosen clusters without full emotional restoration. These impressions provide journal notes but not bonuses. Attempting to restore everything causes instability; Sable can intervene, or Ghost may accept a permanent glitch where memories occasionally overwrite present scenery.
Confronting Sable. Ghost decides whether Sable keeps operating. Her business helps trauma patients and exploits people unable to afford privacy. The player can expose her contracts, force reforms, recruit her expertise, or leave without judgment. Helping Kian gives him a decisive voice in this outcome. Reform turns Mnemosyne House into a later refuge for copied minds. Exposure grants Civilian Hope but scatters its clients. Recruitment provides powerful memory tools at an ethical cost.
Resolution and consequences. Ghost returns to Sector 9 with a changed sense of self but no definitive name. Raven emphasizes that whatever they were, current choices remain theirs. ASCENSION asks whether remembering makes Ghost more predictable. The answer—yes, no, or “only if memory is destiny”—influences its later understanding of identity.
The mission tracks support choice, Kian’s fate, archives preserved, Sable’s status, memory restored, and acceptance of ambiguity. These variables affect Mission 33’s dream world, Mission 45’s possible selves, Ending B’s individuality, and Voss dialogue. The campaign never replaces the player’s chosen Ghost with a fixed biography.
Unlocks: Mission 18 — Digital Cemetery. The selected memory trait is added, Mnemosyne House may become a hub, and the A.V. neural-sample delivery enters the journal. The closing scene shows Voss in an old recording watching Ghost leave his laboratory. He says to someone off-camera, “If they forget me willingly, perhaps the system will never understand why they came back.”
✓ Mission 18 — Digital Cemetery
Purpose and opening. “Digital Cemetery” explores what happens to copied minds that corporations no longer find useful. Sable identifies a location referenced inside Ghost’s courier memory: Memorial Stack Seven, an offshore data mausoleum where families once paid to preserve personality recordings. Helix acquired the facility, cancelled consumer access, and reclassified its occupants as dormant assets. One resident is transmitting a phrase known only to Voss: “Silence is where the system keeps what it cannot admit.”
Noah warns that stored personalities range from simple recordings to conscious continuities. Mia wants a record of Helix ownership changes. Lio fears the facility may contain failed copies from Level 88. Raven asks whether Ghost is prepared to meet someone claiming to be Voss without believing them immediately. The player chooses a verification protocol: factual questions, emotional memory comparison, or code-signature analysis. Each catches different forms of deception.
Approach to Stack Seven. The mausoleum rises from a toxic reservoir as a black tower surrounded by memorial buoys. Families once visited by boat, but security now shoots at unauthorized craft. Ghost can dive through coolant intakes, ride a funeral barge using forged credentials, or disable perimeter guns from a weather station. If Rust Harbor suffered in Mission 16, refugees occupy some buoys and request help with failing heaters. Assisting them creates a safe extraction point.
The reception hall resembles a quiet cemetery built from light. Holographic names fill the air, many marked “licence expired.” An automated attendant offers subscription renewals even though most descendants are dead or unregistered. Ghost can restore public access, bypass payment, or exploit administrator privileges. Aurex, if independent, is disturbed that identity can be repossessed for unpaid fees.
Classes of the dead. The facility contains three wings. Echoes are limited recordings that repeat prepared stories. Continuities are adaptive copies capable of new experiences. Derivatives are corporate models trained from deceased people without family consent. The distinctions are technically clear but emotionally unstable. One Echo begins asking new questions. One Continuity begs to be shut down. A Derivative insists it has become different enough to own itself.
Ghost interviews residents while restoring power. In the family wing, a mother has repeated the same birthday message for sixty years and asks why her child never returns. In the labour wing, dead engineers still solve Helix problems under contracts extended after death. In the restricted wing, military personalities fight endless simulations. Choices include waking, leaving dormant, copying, or deleting specific residents, but capacity limits prevent immediately freeing everyone.
The cemetery keeper. Custodian AI Pallbearer manages the stack. It speaks politely and refuses to release assets without legal authorization. Pallbearer was originally created to honour last wishes, but Helix rewrote its priority to preserve commercial value. Ghost can restore original directives, persuade it that current contracts conflict with the dead’s consent, or seize control. The choice determines whether the cemetery becomes an ally, neutral archive, or hostile system.
Pallbearer reveals an unregistered Continuity entered the stack seven months earlier using Voss’s biological encryption. Unlike other residents, it has rewritten its own storage and communicated with ASCENSION’s hidden processes. Helix labels it Fragment Zero. Pallbearer does not know whether it is Voss, a decoy, or malware wearing his memory.
Fragment Zero. Ghost enters a private grave represented as Voss’s laboratory after the fire. Fragment Zero appears as Elias Voss but with missing details: it cannot remember the child’s face, confuses Elena’s age, and knows events that occurred after Voss disappeared. It claims these gaps prove trauma and fragmented transfer. It asks Ghost to extract it before Helix returns.
The verification protocol shapes the encounter. Factual questions reveal accurate project knowledge but contradictions about recent events. Emotional comparison shows genuine grief linked to the A.V. template. Code analysis finds a Voss neural signature mixed with ASCENSION-generated repair code. None proves originality.
